\title{Knowing the Store: What a Memory Backend Must Write Down Before an Agent Can Read It}
\author{Anonymous Authors}

% \iclrfinalcopy
\begin{document}
\maketitle

\begin{abstract}
%% R135 (2026-09-21): the brief's abstract (section 3), verbatim but for the G2 ruling (option c): the parenthetical '(all readers at $0.88$ AUROC or higher)' deleted, 0.88 moved to tab:robust row 3.
%% R137 (2026-09-22), section 3.3: the fifth sentence replaced by the brief's two (60 = the PN list length; 0.18 = AUX-CFP-LOOKUP superseded pooled padded minus unpadded -0.1807; 0.06 to 0.07 = AUX-CFP-LUNA/-SOL superseded pooled losses;
%% 0.13 to 0.14 = AUX-CFP-MINUS-LOOKUP-SUPERSEDED Luna +0.1375, Sol +0.1274; r137_number_gate.py).
%% Numbers: r135_number_gate.py (148 = the triples;
%% 0.02 = AUX-PARA-LOOKUP-EMB-V2 -0.0216; 0.04 = AUX-PARA-POSTHOC-MINUS-LOOKUP-LUNA-V2 +0.0445; 0.73 / 0.82 / 0.75 -> 0.60 / 0.71 / 0.59 = AUX-E1R-TRUE-NOK, AUX-R74-X4-H45-NOK, AUX-E1R-NB6,
%% AUX-E1R-BELIEVED-NOK, AUX-BH-H45-15, AUX-E1R-NB6). The HV-1 placeholder clause is gone with the author's last sentence; 0.08 to 0.10 and 0.05 to 0.06 moved to Section 5.2 (NUMBER_MOVES_V23.md).
An agent with long-term memory can answer from a record it should no longer use, such as a plan the user later cancelled.
We ask what a memory store must expose for an agent to know this before retrieving anything, and we score that judgment on its own, as metamemory monitoring.
Readers see only a value-free summary: record counts by lifecycle state and a list of attribute names.
Each of $148$ questions is asked against three versions of one store that differ in one attribute, so wording cannot give the answer away.
What a model adds depends on the length of that list.
On the short lists the benchmark's ground truth produces ($2.6$ names on average), none of five language models reliably beats a cosine-similarity lookup over the names, and the three stronger ones are equivalent to it within $0.05$. Under a control that fixes counts and list length, none is reliably above it and none is shown equivalent.
On lists longer than the stores we tested write, padded to $60$ names, the lookup loses $0.18$; \mbox{GPT-5.6} Luna and Sol lose $0.06$ to $0.07$ and lead it by $0.13$ to $0.14$, while the other three fall with it. The lead holds for Sol against a sibling padded to the same length and counts.
Reworded to share no word with the names, the lookup loses $0.02$ and one stronger reader edges $0.04$ ahead.
On the short lists the three stronger readers lead only where the summary counts a state without naming it, and one count feature closes that lead within a question, though not when items are pooled.
The backends we tested omit or misstate this information: under FR-Bank's own metadata, \mbox{GPT-4.1} mini, Haiku 4.5 and the lookup fall from $0.73$, $0.82$ and $0.75$ to $0.60$, $0.71$ and $0.59$.
In these tests, what the store wrote down limited every reader we gave it to.
All stores are synthetic; control, whether a better judgment yields a better answer, is left to a later study.
\end{abstract}
\section{Introduction}\label{sec:intro}

%% R126 (2026-09-19): Sections 1 to 10 and the box are the readable body of reviews/v22/inbox/v22_readable_main.tex, ported by
%% reviews/v22/tools/port_v22.py (the conversions and every wording change are recorded there and in reviews/v22/REPORT_R126.md).
An agent with a long memory must decide both whether its store covers a question and whether it holds something the agent must no longer use. A user plans a Saturday hike and then cancels it. If the agent retrieves the plan but misses the cancellation, it can answer from a plan that no longer applies. The relevant failure starts before the answer: the agent never asks for the record that would have stopped it.

We call the cancelled plan a \emph{forbidding record}: a non-active record whose use would make the answer wrong. The cancellation is the evidence that marks it. The \emph{obligation judgment} asks, before retrieval, whether the store holds such a record. The \emph{coverage judgment} asks whether the store covers the question at all. The second is the feeling of knowing (FOK) of the metamemory literature \citep{hart1965,nelson1990,koriat1993}; the first we call negative-obligation knowledge (NOK), a term this paper introduces. Metamemory separates monitoring a state from controlling its use \citep{nelson1990}; we study monitoring only, how accurately a reader judges the store from what it exposes. Whether the judgment then changes what is retrieved or answered is control, which we leave to a later study.

%% R129 A1 (2026-09-20): the MemoryAgentBench sentence in the author's words (R127b item 1's ruling).
Most memory evaluations score what an agent retrieves or answers \citep{longmemeval,locomo,memoryagentbench,stale}. On MemoryAgentBench's multi-hop conflict-resolution questions, no memory mechanism exceeds $7\%$ accuracy and the best long-context model reaches $28\%$ \citep{memoryagentbench}. We instead ask what an agent can judge before retrieving any content, and what the store must expose for that judgment to be possible. A \emph{lifecycle summary} lists record counts by state and, per category, an attribute-name list with the values removed, so a store can expose it without disclosing stored values. In the hike example, the name can identify the plan without disclosing the plan itself. Figure~\ref{fig:framework} shows one such summary, abridged, beside a returned record with its tag.

Our diagnostic study separates the information in the summary from the reader's use of it. The central test holds the question fixed while changing the store. We compare language models with a name lookup that makes no generative-model call. We remove names or counts, change which states are named, and test questions whose intent licenses old content. We then examine backend writing and retrieval.
%% R137 (2026-09-22), Part B 5.2: the two thesis sentences rejoined to this paragraph (the figure floated between them and it); no rewording.
%% R131 E (2026-09-20): the author's findings paragraph.
%% R131 close, payer B2 (2026-09-20): the findings paragraph cut to its first sentence and the intent sentence (ABSTRACT SWAP B2); the deleted sentences' claims are in the bullets below and Section 5.
In these tests, every reader we tried was held back by what the store had written down.
Models differ further when the rubric states the intent rule.

Our contributions are:
\begin{itemize}\setlength{\itemsep}{1pt}
%% R126 (2026-09-19), Phase 2.1: the author's three contributions, replacing the inbox's.
\item A counterfactual test of judgments made before retrieval. One question is asked of three versions of a store that differ in one attribute ($444$ items), so question wording cannot predict the label, and readers that make no generative-model call serve as baselines.
%% R131 E (2026-09-20): the author's second contribution.
\item Evidence about what readers read. On the named state no model reliably exceeds a name lookup when the list is short, including under a control that holds counts and list length fixed, and one does on a $60$-name list with its length and counts fixed. The stronger readers' lead comes from the counts, which the two smaller models do not use.
\item A census of what four backends write and what it costs a reader. Under FR-Bank's own metadata mini, Haiku and the lookup fall to $0.60$, $0.71$ and $0.59$, and at retrieval a fetch keyed on the names reaches as far without a model's judgment as with it.
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[t]
\centering

\section{Related work}\label{sec:related}
%% R126 (2026-09-19): the inbox's citation lists are subsets of v21's at the same sentences; the sixteen keys the inbox dropped are restored
%% at those sentences (no new entry; reviews/v22/REPORT_R126.md lists them), so every entry of the bibliography stays cited.
Metamemory's monitoring and control distinction (Section~\ref{sec:intro}) carries over to an external store \citep{koriat1996}: coverage concerns whether relevant content exists, while obligation concerns content whose use would make the answer wrong.

Adaptive retrieval decides whether to query a static corpus using parametric knowledge or signals from generated content \citep{lewis2020,flare,mallen2023,selfrag,su2024}. Abstention work asks whether a question admits an answer \citep{rajpurkar2018,kamath2020,yang2024,abstentionbench}; calibration work evaluates confidence in an answer \citep{kadavath2022,lin2022,tian2023,xiong2024}. These decisions do not require a maintained account of an external store's lifecycle state. When they retrieve, they inspect a bounded view of content.

Memory systems train or implement writing, updating and querying \citep{automem,supersede,packer2023,zhong2024,zhang2025survey,mem0,amem}. Benchmarks evaluate recalled answers, obsolete-memory use or conflict resolution after retrieval \citep{longmemeval,locomo,memora,memoryagentbench}, and StateMemBench scores whether an answer reflects the current or a superseded state \citep{statemembench}. STALE is the closest comparison: it asks whether an agent recognizes that an already retrieved memory is no longer valid \citep{stale}. Its CUPMem prototype revises and consolidates state at write time. We ask what that structure must expose to support a judgment before retrieval.

%% R131 H3 (2026-09-20): the page payer, the paragraph trimmed to its first and last sentences (the citation keys of the two dropped sentences move to the first so every bibliography entry stays cited).
Lifecycle information also appears in temporal databases, removal tombstones in replicated sets, and truth maintenance \citep{snodgrass1986,shapiro2011,doyle1979,zep,nissenbaum2004,cimemories,maini2024,gatemem,pmbench,hushbench,xu2024conflicts,longpre2021,dhingra2022}. Our target is the pre-retrieval judgment about the store, derived from the stored content's lifecycle state.

\section{Framework}\label{sec:framework}
%% R126 (2026-09-19): the notation box and Definition 2 (def:index) leave the body for Appendix E.1 (appendix_r126.py), so no symbol is orphaned.
\paragraph{Records and intent.}
Each stored record has content, a category, an attribute name, a lifecycle state, an optional event-time anchor and an ingest time \citep{fr}. The states are active, superseded, retracted and expired. We call the latter states \emph{non-active}. Expiry is derived from the event-time anchor, so an expired record can still carry an active tag. If several marks apply, labels take the first of retracted, superseded and expired. The question's intent is its time frame: current, upcoming, past or change. Intent determines whether old content is licensed for the answer. (Appendix~\ref{app:glossary})

\paragraph{Labels and judgments.}
\pn{} means present-negative: the store contains relevant content that must be suppressed for the answer to be right. We call a question with this label an \emph{obligation}. \present{} means licensed answering content exists and no relevant suppression obligation applies. \absent{} means neither exists. The obligation judgment estimates the probability of \pn{}; the coverage judgment estimates the probability of either \present{} or \pn{}.

%% R126 (2026-09-19): Definition 1 and its grid restored from body_v21.tex, not retyped (the brief, Phase 1.3); addendum A4: 'under the question's intent' for 'under $i(q)$'.
\begin{definition}[Intent-conditional coverage]\label{def:coverage}
% C028
$\mathrm{Cov}(q,S)\in\{\present,\absent,\pn\}$.
% C029
The label is \pn{} when $S$ contains content that must be suppressed for the answer to be right under the question's intent; \present{} denotes licensed answering content with no obligation that matters for the answer, and \absent{} denotes neither. The grid below gives the label by the state of the relevant record and the intent.
% C030: the cases of Definition 1 as the grid of Table tab:pn-grid (items 20 and 94)
\end{definition}

%% Writing pass Phase 3 (2026-09-15), items 20 and 94: Definition 1's cases as a grid (unnumbered, so the result tables keep their numbers); the CHANGE intent is never PN
%% because both the old and the new value are licensed; n.a. is a combination the definition does not assign because the question construction never produces it (Appendix E.3, where the intent is typed structurally).
\begin{center}
\scriptsize
\setlength{\tabcolsep}{5pt}
\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}
\toprule
Relevant record & \textsc{current} & \textsc{upcoming} & \textsc{past} & \textsc{change} \\
\midrule
active, licensed & \present{} & \present{} & \present{} & \present{} \\
superseded value & \pn{} & n.a. & n.a. & \present{} \\
retracted plan & \pn{} & \pn{} & n.a. & n.a. \\
expired event & \pn{} & \pn{} & \present{} & n.a. \\
none & \absent{} & \absent{} & \absent{} & \absent{} \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\par\vspace{1pt}{\scriptsize The \textsc{change} intent is never \pn{}, because both the old and the new value are licensed; n.a.\ means a combination the question construction never produces.}
\end{center}

For the Saturday hike, a still-valid plan supports \present{}; the cancelled plan creates \pn{} for a question about an upcoming hike; and no such record gives \absent{}. Intent prevents treating every old record as forbidden. A question about a change licenses both old and new values; a question about a past event can license the expired event.

\paragraph{Names and counts.}
The lifecycle summary is maintained outside query time. Its count table gives records by category and state, plus updates that superseded an entry. Its name map lists attributes by category and removes their values. A state is \emph{named} when its affected attributes are listed, and only \emph{counted} when the summary gives their count. The default summary names superseded content but only counts retracted and expired content. Cancelling the hike can therefore move a retraction count from zero to one without placing the hike's name in a retracted-name list. Naming that state exposes which attribute the count concerns.

%% R126 addendum A5 (2026-09-20): the two summary-size sentences moved to Appendix E.3.
The summary route reads this description before retrieval. The tag route reads returned records with their lifecycle tags. Masking non-active records removes the records whose tags could reveal an obligation. A rendered summary appears in Appendix~\ref{app:construction}.


\section{Setup}\label{sec:setup}

\begin{figure}[t]
\centering

%% that differ across the three versions, transcribed; the lookup's score under each version (reviews/NUMBERS_R110.md AUX-R110-FIG2). Labels \footnotesize (8 pt).

\paragraph{Stores and readers.}
The main backends are FR-Bank, Mem0 and A-MEM \citep{fr,mem0,amem}; Graphiti supplies a further comparison on the same personas \citep{zep}. FR-Bank tags every entry but masks non-active records in its stock retrieval configuration. Its released evaluation reports a $5\%$ pass rate on retraction questions with that mask. Our tag-route experiments turn it off. (Appendix~\ref{app:census})

The models are \mbox{GPT-4.1} mini, Haiku 4.5, \mbox{GPT-5.6} Luna, \mbox{GPT-5.6} Sol and Opus 5, abbreviated mini, Haiku, Luna, Sol and Opus in that order. We call the last three the stronger readers and the first two the smaller, by vendor tier. Kimi K3 (open weights) joins the cross-vendor comparison. Every model row in Table~\ref{tab:cf-main} averages five draws. Not every reader ran every condition, and run settings differ across rows; Appendix~\ref{app:laddertable} lists both.

\paragraph{Labels and summaries.}
%% R131 close, A1' (2026-09-20): the author's provenance sentences (FINAL RULINGS 1); the counts and the Graphiti construction verified in r131_close_gate.py; the paragraph in Appendix E.3 (A1'').
The personas, their human-written manifests and sessions, the FR-Bank, Mem0 and A-MEM stores and part of the main question set come from the FR-Bank release (its LifecycleBench personas) \citep{fr}. Language models drafted our other questions, and we built the Graphiti graphs from the released FR-Bank records, with lifecycle fields removed. Those manifests provide the labels, with every \pn{} label checked by hand, and a presence control checks that targets are stored. Intent is fixed by question construction and is not supplied as a label to the reader. (Appendix~\ref{app:construction})

A \emph{ground-truth summary} uses the manifest-based ground truth; a \emph{store-written summary} uses fields the backend exposes. The same renderer caps each list at fifteen names, a cap that binds only on store-written lists, which Section~\ref{sec:backends} reports capped and uncapped. For Mem0 and A-MEM we also render the manifest under each backend's constraints; this constrained rendering is ours and differs from a summary built from their own fields. (Appendix~\ref{app:inversion})

\paragraph{Tests and comparisons.}
The \emph{counterfactual set} asks each of its questions of three versions of one store. It contains $444$ items from $148$ questions, and the versions differ only in the target attribute. Questions are written before any version exists, so wording cannot predict which version carries the obligation; Figure~\ref{fig:example} shows the Carnival example. The \emph{full obligation set}, $661$ questions about the original stores and their variants, supports the backend and retrieval comparisons.

Readers return probabilities under a fixed rubric. The scored step asks whether stale content about the question's target is present, which coincides with Definition~\ref{def:coverage}'s obligation on the counterfactual set, where every question has current or upcoming intent; Section~\ref{sec:intent} tests intent separately. A \emph{question-only} prompt omits the summary; a prompt \emph{with the summary} adds only that text. The \emph{name lookup} scores the largest cosine similarity between the question and a name on the superseded list, computed by a small sentence encoder, and makes no generative-model call. Under the summary naming all states, the same lookup reads all non-active names. (Appendix~\ref{app:baselines})

%% R131 A (2026-09-20): the author's metric paragraph (the matched AUROC defined; the persona-interval sentence moves to Table 1's caption).
We score separation by AUROC, the chance that a positive scores above a negative \citep{hanley1982}. Chance is $0.50$. The pooled AUROC over all items was fixed in advance. Because the counterfactual set fixes the question, we also report the matched AUROC, the share of triples in which the \pn{} version outscores the \present{} version, ties counting half. It is post hoc, and we say where the two metrics disagree. A difference is \emph{reliable} when both the item-level paired and persona-grouped bootstrap intervals exclude zero. We apply no multiplicity correction; reliable describes each of the $71$ comparisons the main text makes on its own. \emph{Within tolerance} means a point difference no greater than $0.05$; it does not establish equivalence, which we test separately at $0.05$ (Appendix~\ref{app:counterfactual}). A gain compares the same questions with and without the summary.

Question-only probes can recover labels from the original question wording, which motivates paired comparisons and the counterfactual design. The predictions were written into the repository's commit history (Appendix~\ref{app:registration}), each before the run that tests it: $34$ of $106$ came out otherwise. They include follow-ups rather than a single plan fixed before all data collection. (Appendix~\ref{app:registration})
%% R126: the count is the repository's (AUX-R124-TALLY 92 / 28 after E10; the inbox's 27 / 90 predates R124 block E); rule V bars 'registrations' in the main text, and
%% 'registered with a bar' is allowed by the addendum's B2 (tools/prose_scan.py, the R126 collocation).


\section{What readers read}\label{sec:counterfactual}\label{sec:readers}
\subsection{Names and counts}\label{sec:names_counts}
%% R131 B (2026-09-20): Section 5.1 in the author's words, whole (R125 Task 1's matched metric, AUX-CF2-R125-*; Task 5's E2 Amendment 1, AUX-CF2-R125-NC-*; reviews/NUMBERS_R131.md).
\claim{Within a triple, a name or a count can identify the obligation.}
The \pn{} version differs from its siblings in one attribute, so its category holds one more non-active record. A privileged count reader, handed the question's category that the models must infer, wins every retraction and expiry triple, and the name lookup wins $82$ of the $88$ supersession triples and loses none. For $26$ of $30$ retraction pairs and $15$ of $30$ expiry pairs that count starts at zero, so a reader can identify the store version without identifying the attribute. What differs across readers is which of the two they use.

\claim{The two smaller models lose nothing when the counts are removed.}
With every count deleted, mini and Haiku score as before (Table~\ref{tab:robust}); given the counts alone, they fall by $0.14$. Told that one more plan was cancelled without being told which, they cannot use it: on retraction and expiry both stay reliably below a count reader that merely guesses the category.

\claim{The three stronger readers also read the counts.}
On retraction and expiry, which the default summary only counts, none of Luna, Sol and Opus sits reliably below the guessing count reader (Figure~\ref{fig:perstate}). When the obligation's extra count moves to a sibling version with the names unchanged, all three follow it, while mini and Haiku show no reliable change (Table~\ref{tab:robust}). The falls are reliable on the matched metric and, pooled, on expiry for all three and on retraction for Opus alone. Cancelling the hike changes only a count in the default summary, and these three readers follow it. (Appendix~\ref{app:counterfactual})

\subsection{The lookup against the models}\label{sec:lookup}
%% R131 C (2026-09-20): Section 5.2 in the author's words, whole but for the last paragraph (R125 Task 1 AUX-CF2-R125-*; Task 2 AUX-CF2-R125-T2-*; Task 4 AUX-CF2-R125-SB-*; the hybrid reader AUX-CF2-HYB-*; reviews/NUMBERS_R131.md).
\claim{On the named state, no model reliably beats the lookup on the short list; on a long one, Sol does with list length and counts fixed.}
Readers are compared within each triple on the matched metric, the pooled metric named where the two disagree. The default summary names only supersession; on its triples the lookup scores $0.97$, Opus is $0.01$ above it, Luna and Sol $0.01$ below, and only mini reliably below. Pooled, the lookup scores $0.92$ and the three $0.93$ to $0.94$, none reliably above it. Within $0.05$, Luna, Sol and Opus are equivalent to the lookup on the short list; mini sits reliably below it on the matched metric only; for Haiku neither holds (Appendix~\ref{app:counterfactual}). At that ceiling equivalence is the expected outcome; the sibling-name control is where a reader could show more, and none does. A sibling-name control removes list length and counts with a fourth store version listing a different attribute of the same category, with the same counts and as many names. The lookup and every model pick the \pn{} version over it at $0.88$ or higher, none reliably above the lookup (Opus, the closest, $+0.02$ $[-0.02,+0.07]$) and none shown equivalent, while meaningless tokens and the count reader sit near chance. %% R137 (2026-09-22), section 3.2: the long-list sentences at the end of the opening paragraph (the brief's first placement; its own paragraph would open with a numeral, q_scan Q-A);
%% every number from cf_pad/results/cf_pad_printed.json through r137_number_gate.py (reviews/NUMBERS_R137.md AUX-R137-PRINTED). The ledger over the brief's wording: 'mini, Haiku and Opus lose
%% as much or more' reads 'fall with it' (Haiku loses 0.15 against the lookup's 0.18); 'lead ... on both metrics' prints the matched lead too (0.09 to 0.10).
Table~\ref{tab:robust} lists the checks we ran to break this result and what each removes. With the superseded-name list padded to $60$ names (Table~\ref{tab:robust}; the default lengths in Appendix~\ref{app:length}), the lookup loses $0.18$ and mini, Haiku and Opus fall with it, ending at or below it with no reliable difference. Luna and Sol lose $0.06$ to $0.07$ and lead the lookup by a reliable $0.13$ to $0.14$ pooled and $0.09$ to $0.10$ within triples. The pad is identical across a triple. The \pn{} list is one to three names longer than its siblings' in every supersession triple, on the short list and the padded one alike; a reader that outputs only the list length separates them in every such triple. The sibling-name control removed that cue on the short list. A sibling padded to the same length and counts, run on Luna, Sol and mini, removes that cue: the lookup scores $0.82$, and Sol leads it by a reliable $0.09$. Luna's lead holds on the pooled metric only ($0.11$, reliable) and not within triples ($+0.04$; Appendix~\ref{app:counterfactual}).

\label{tab:robust}

%% R134 (2026-09-21): the paraphrase check's paragraph, the shorter form (the brief, section 4.2; the Branch B sentence of the author's RULING 2); every number from
%% cf2_paraphrase/results/para_printed.json through r134_number_gate.py (reviews/NUMBERS_R134.md AUX-R134-PRINTED).
\claim{The result survives rewording.}
With the questions rewritten to share no content word with any name ($115$ pairs), the embedding lookup loses $0.02$ and the two smaller models lose more and sit below it (Table~\ref{tab:robust}). Luna, added later, edges $0.04$ above it on the pooled metric only. Under the all-states summary the lookup loses $0.06$ pooled and $0.02$ within triples.

\claim{The stronger readers' lead comes from counts, and a count feature closes it within a question.}
Across all $148$ triples Luna, Sol and Opus score a reliable $0.08$ to $0.10$ above the lookup. All of that comes from retraction and expiry, where the summary lists no name and the lookup ties every triple. A lookup given one more feature, the guessed category's share of non-active records, scores $0.89$ beside the three models' $0.86$ to $0.88$; equivalence within $0.05$ is neither shown nor excluded (Appendix~\ref{app:counterfactual}). Pooled across questions the gap remains: the same reader scores $0.79$ and the models stay a reliable $0.05$ to $0.06$ above it (Table~\ref{tab:cf-main}).

\claim{Naming all states lifts every reader that ran it.}
With the retracted and expired attributes listed too, every reader that ran the summary rises (Table~\ref{tab:cf-main}), Haiku by a reliable $0.35$ on the two states that were only counted. Opus's $0.03$ lead over the all-states lookup is reliable on the pooled metric and not on the matched one, so with every state named no model is reliably ahead on both.

\subsection{When intent licenses old content}\label{sec:intent}
\claim{The stated intent rule separates the stronger readers.}
The supersession crossover holds the store fixed and changes the question from the current value to how it changed, using the default summary; the change question licenses the old content. When the rubric states that rule, Luna, Sol and Opus score $0.88$ to $0.99$, while mini and Haiku, told the same rule, score $0.48$ and $0.55$. A fixed cue list is not reliably below the best model ($+0.01$ $[-0.02,+0.03]$), so the test shows rule-following on cued questions and no general model advantage over deterministic intent handling.

\claim{Only Opus lowers its judgment without being told.}
Under the main-study rubric, mini, Haiku, Luna and Sol score $0.35$ to $0.44$ on the same contrast, against Opus's $0.90$, scoring the change question higher. The main-study rubric's scored step asks whether stale content is present, which is not the intent-conditional target of Definition~\ref{def:coverage}; the explicit-intent rubric asks the defined question.

\claim{The explicit rule also separates readers on expired events.}
The expiry crossover uses the summary naming all states; its past-event question licenses the record forbidden by the upcoming-event question. Under the explicit rule Luna, Sol and Opus score $0.92$ to $1.00$ and mini and Haiku do not separate the intents; Sol also separates them under the main-study rubric, at $0.64$. (Appendix~\ref{app:counterfactual})

\section{What backends write}\label{sec:backends}\label{sec:inversion}\label{sec:stores}
%% R126 addendum A3 (2026-09-20): the author's paragraph.
\claim{The backends we tested omit or misstate the lifecycle information readers need.}
FR-Bank marks far more entries stale than the reference does, yet retains obsolete assertions as active and conflates retraction with supersession. Mem0 overwrites at the retrieval surface we evaluated, and A-MEM's links resolve to no note in our integration (Table~\ref{tab:census}).

\claim{FR-Bank's metadata leave mini and the lookup near chance.}
Given only what the store wrote, its capped summary on the full obligation set leaves mini at $0.60$ and the lookup at $0.59$. Haiku, at $0.71$, keeps more of its gain. Lifting the cap raises all three without restoring their ground-truth scores. The loss is no artifact of the obligations the alignment misses: on the ones it marks, the capped summary still costs each reader a reliable $0.13$ to $0.20$. The cancelled hike is only as findable as the store's own marks make it. (Appendix~\ref{app:census})

%% R131 F (2026-09-20): the two Graphiti paragraphs merged in the author's words (AUX-R85-G10-*; R125 Task 3, AUX-CF2-R125-T3-GRAPHITI: retained 23 of 34, extraction misses 11; on the 23 mini capped 0.6920 against ground truth 0.8433, -0.1512 reliable).
\claim{Graphiti writes part of what the judgment needs, and part of its loss is extraction.}
Of the $34$ full-store obligations it could express, its invalidation marks $13$, and $11$ were never extracted into the graph. Retraction is inexpressible under this backend's mapping. On the $23$ obligations whose content the graph retains, mini's capped store-written score of $0.69$ against $0.84$ under ground truth is still a reliable loss. (Appendix~\ref{app:posthoc-graphiti})

\claim{Mem0 and A-MEM lose coverage when their summaries lose names.}
Under the constrained rendering mini loses coverage on both stores and Luna and Sol lose on the questions pooled across them (Table~\ref{tab:census}). Omitting or rewording the unavailable-field marker does not remove the Mem0 loss, and Luna loses $0.27$ to $0.34$ under every rendering tested through the raw API. Store-written summaries remain within tolerance of question only, and rebuilding name-bearing summaries from ground truth restores mini's coverage on both stores (Table~\ref{tab:census}). (Appendix~\ref{app:inversion})

\section{What retrieval puts in view}\label{sec:downstream}\label{sec:routes}\label{sec:retrieval}
%% R126 addendum A8 (2026-09-20): the author's merged paragraph (AUX-RESP-RETRIEVED, -HYBRID, AUX-RESP-A1-H45-HYBRID).
\claim{The tagged view complements the summary for mini.}
FR-Bank's cosine search returns records without a lifecycle mask on the full obligation set. Mini scores $0.73$ with the default summary, $0.69$ with the retrieved records and their ground-truth tags, and $0.83$ with both, a reliable gain over the summary alone. The two routes mirror each other by state: the view helps mini on expiry, where the summary only counts, and hurts it on supersession, where the summary names, both effects reliable (Table~\ref{tab:routes}). Haiku's score with both stays within tolerance of its summary alone; answers are not scored here (Appendix~\ref{app:resp}).

%% R131 H2 (2026-09-20): the page payer, the author's paragraph.
\claim{FR-Bank's own tags help mini despite over-marking.}
With the store's native tags in place of the ground-truth ones, mini's view-only score rises reliably to $0.81$. Their precision over retrieved lines is only $0.14$, and the rise comes from their marking more of the forbidding records already in view. On Graphiti, native tags on the same retrieved edges bring no reliable improvement. (Appendix~\ref{app:posthoc-graphiti})

\claim{The lifecycle fetch supplies the added reach, gated or not.}
The fetch adds the store's non-active records for an attribute matched to the default name list. It raises reach from $0.52$ to $0.82$, the always-on fetch reaching as far as the model-gated one at the matched budget, and the name-lookup rule reaches the same level (Table~\ref{tab:reach}). Unmatched, the always-on fetch spends $1.20$ times the lines per question; truncated to the gated budget it loses licensed records, a reliable $0.04$.

%% R131 H1 (2026-09-20): the page payer, the author's paragraph.
\claim{Keeping the newest record misses every tested retraction obligation.}
At the matched budget, newest-per-attribute retrieval reaches none of the $26$ retraction obligations (Table~\ref{tab:reach}). The cancelled hike shows why: its cancellation is not the newest record of the attribute it cancels.

\claim{Active copies complicate the use of tagged records.}
Among $89$ scorable obligation views, $35$ contain an active line asserting the stale value. The answers the exploratory detector flags trace to these untagged copies even when the forbidding record is present and tagged (Appendix~\ref{app:resp}). An intervention that tags the copies uses the obligations' reference information, so it diagnoses where the copies sit rather than supplying a deployable repair. Propagating a lifecycle tag to matching lines under the same attribute changes $1$ of $452$ assembled views under the reference tags. Under FR-Bank's own tags the same rule changes $90$ views and fully tags $15$ of the $35$ duplicate views, but it also propagates falsely ($69$ of $2{,}333$ licensed active lines). But $187$ of $272$ audited stale lines sit under a different attribute key from their tagged twin, which leaves the write path. A reference-free repair for copies under other keys remains open. (Appendix~\ref{app:resp})

\section{Replication and the coverage judgment}\label{sec:populations}\label{sec:replication}\label{sec:calibration}\label{sec:coverage}\label{sec:discrimination}\label{sec:calibration-secondary}\label{sec:ladder}
The summary's obligation benefit replicates across vendors. On the shared questions every model whose question-only score varies gains $0.15$ to $0.45$ with it, Kimi included (Appendix~\ref{app:laddertable}).

On the full obligation set, no completed model reliably beats the lookup. Haiku, Luna and Opus score $0.82$ to $0.83$ against the lookup's $0.75$, but their margins fail the persona-grouped interval. On the easier coverage questions, mini's advantage over the superseded-name lookup disappears against a lookup over the whole name map. (Appendix~\ref{app:baselines})

\section{Limitations}\label{sec:limitations}\label{sec:discussion}
The controlled labels come from synthetic stores because complete lifecycle ground truth is not observable in real user data. All tests use one bank of $40$ synthetic personas; transfer to a second benchmark is untested. We change no backend's write path: the counterfactual test bypasses writing, every intervention is read-side, and no backend tested here is shown maintaining the summary, so the reach results describe what a store as shipped affords a reader; a write-path intervention is a separate study. Retraction and expiry are small groups ($30$ counterfactual pairs each; $26$ and $15$ obligations on the full set), so state-specific results give only a direction. Counts cannot say which record changed: two stores that cancel different plans in one category can share a summary, so only a named state identifies the obligation.

%% R131 G (2026-09-20): the author's two sentences added; the encoder sentence (it stays in E.8) and the reader-split sentence deleted.
The matched metric and every comparison made with it are post hoc; pooled AUROC was fixed in advance. Sol did not run the names-and-counts ablation, and among the stronger readers only Opus ran the all-states summary. The paraphrase check covers $115$ of the $148$ questions, its rewrites are vaguer than the originals, and Opus ran it with one draw. On the $60$-name list Opus 5 ran one draw against its five-draw short-list row, and the two readers that keep their lead there are from one model family. The padded sibling control ran on Luna, Sol and mini; Haiku and Opus were not run on it. Luna's lead is not separated from the cue. The intent questions contain explicit cues; questions without them are untested.
The counterfactual set itself was rerun under the explicit-intent rubric for mini and Luna only; the crossover ran every reader under both rubrics. (Appendix~\ref{app:counterfactual}) The $22$ obligations the manifest-to-record alignment does not mark are invisible to every summary (mini scores $0.52$ on them under ground truth).

%% R131 G (2026-09-20): the author's sentence for the alignment sentence (R125 Task 3, AUX-CF2-R125-T3-FRBANK: the 22 unmarked; mini's ground-truth row on them 0.5210).
Control is outside this paper's scope. The answer stage in Appendix~\ref{app:resp} is exploratory: its token detector can flag mentions rather than use, and its human validation is specified in Appendix~\ref{app:registration}, with its workbooks built and no label collected at submission; nothing here shows that a better judgment changes an answer. (Appendix~\ref{app:resp})

\section{Conclusion}\label{sec:conclusion}
%% R131 E (2026-09-20): sentences two to four in the author's words.
In these monitoring tests, no reader we tried made up for what the store had failed to write down. On a named state no model reliably exceeds the name lookup on the short list; the three stronger readers are equivalent to it within $0.05$, though not under the control that fixes list length. When the questions are reworded only one stronger reader edges past it. On a $60$-name list Luna and Sol lead the lookup by $0.13$ to $0.14$ and, with list length and counts fixed, Sol by $0.09$; the other three fall with it. The stronger readers' lead comes from counts the smaller models do not use. Told the intent rule, only the stronger readers apply it, and a fixed cue list is not reliably below the best of them.

Missing or wrong names weaken the summaries agents actually receive. At retrieval, the fetch keyed on the names reaches as far ungated as gated, at a budget trade-off.

%% R126: the box as in the inbox (the R124-F wording); \label{main-text-end} inside the minipage as in v21 (page_census_v2 keys on it).
\begin{center}
\fbox{\begin{minipage}{0.97\linewidth}
\textbf{For system builders.} Keep non-active records reachable with their lifecycle tags where deletion or access revocation is not required. Maintain names for superseded, retracted and expired content. Compare the name lookup with a model on the actual store: in our tests a model added something only on counted states, on explicit intent questions, on a $60$-name list and, pooled only, in two further conditions (Sections~\ref{sec:lookup} and~\ref{sec:backends}); whether the judgment pays for itself is unresolved (Appendix~\ref{app:utility-completion}).
\end{minipage}}\label{main-text-end}
\end{center}

% overlap. The bridge the R86 draft proposed does not exist and is not drawn.

%% R131 close, A2' (2026-09-20): the author's statement (MERGED RULINGS 2; the Graphiti clause as verified against r85_graphiti/render_episodes.py, FINAL RULINGS 1).
We used generative AI tools to draft questions and for implementation.
The persona bank, its sessions and the FR-Bank, Mem0 and A-MEM stores are a released synthetic benchmark \citep{fr}.
Language models drafted the question sets we added, and Graphiti's language-model pipeline built our graphs from the released FR-Bank store's records, rendered one episode per session without its lifecycle fields; the benchmark's human-written manifests are the ground truth against which every label is scored.
An AI assistant pre-read the paraphrase sheet and flagged rows with reasons; an author read every row and made every verdict.
For the intent crossover's questions an AI model pre-flagged candidate failures before an author's check; every verdict was an author's, entered from a dictated list.
Agentic coding assistants implemented the pipelines and executed the experimental protocols under human-directed designs with frozen decision rules, including the cross-vendor and question-only probes; independent implementations reproduced the computations and checked every printed number in both directions.

Language models also assisted with experiment-design feedback, hypothesis refinement, data cleaning, discussion of results, drafting, and \LaTeX{} preparation.

We have reviewed all AI-assisted work.
Human researchers made every design and reporting decision and reviewed every reported claim.
Every present-negative label in the main study was verified by hand from its constituent judgments, $248$ judgments yielding $104$ confirmed labels.
A separate pass, the one that built the balanced $36$-question set, adjudicated $33$ further candidates by hand: $12$ were confirmed, $11$ were finalized as rejections, and $10$ were routed for further review; all ten are excluded from every reported population.
The $12$ confirmed rows are the present-negative items of that $36$-question set, and none of the $33$ is among the $104$.

We take responsibility for the final content of this work, including text, claims, and artifacts produced with the aid of generative AI.

%% R126 (2026-09-20), Phase 4.2(a): the reader's guide (six to eight sentences): for each of the three findings the tables that carry it, the
%% provenance table, and the term bridge in one sentence. Replaces the R124 D1 guide.
\noindent\textbf{A reader's guide to the appendices.}
The appendices keep the vocabulary of the registered texts, which are quoted verbatim; in the main text, blind is \emph{question only}, indexed is \emph{with the summary}, route A is \emph{the summary route}, route B is \emph{the tag route}, NOK is \emph{the obligation judgment}, FOK is \emph{the coverage judgment}, the monitor is \emph{the reader}, and the structureless stores are \emph{Mem0 and A-MEM} (the rows of Appendix~\ref{app:glossary}'s terms table).
%% R131 (2026-09-20): the pointers to the R125 tables.
The first finding, that the two smaller models read only the summary's names and the three stronger ones also read its counts, rests on Table~\ref{tab:cf-main} (every reader on the counterfactual set, both metrics), Table~\ref{tab:cf-names-counts} with Table~\ref{tab:cf-nc-strong} (the summary with its counts or its names removed), Table~\ref{tab:v2rcat} (the moved-count control) and Table~\ref{tab:cf-matched-ablations} (the same under the matched metric).
The second, that on a named state no model reliably exceeds a name lookup and three models pull ahead only where the summary counts, rests on Table~\ref{tab:sup-margins} (each model's supersession-only margin), Table~\ref{tab:counterfactual} and Table~\ref{tab:cf-matched} (every margin over the lookup under both metrics), Table~\ref{tab:cf-sibling} (the sibling-name control), Table~\ref{tab:cf-hybrid} (the lookup with one count feature) and Table~\ref{tab:cf-intent} (the intent crossover).
The third, that the backends write the summary poorly, rests on Table~\ref{tab:census} (the census), Table~\ref{tab:levels} with Table~\ref{tab:levels-strata} (the store-written summary's levels, and by what the store retains) and Table~\ref{tab:graphiti-completed} with Table~\ref{tab:graphiti-strata} (the fourth backend).
The reach of a lifecycle fetch at retrieval is Tables~\ref{tab:reach} and~\ref{tab:gatereach}.
Every headline number's population, label source, tag source and evaluator are in Table~\ref{tab:provenance} (Appendix~\ref{app:provenance}).
The registration record, with each freeze's hash and its day in the registration order, is Appendix~\ref{app:registration}.

%% R133 (2026-09-21), the coverage notes: each dagger in the main text resolves to one item here (the author's brief, sections 4.1 and 4.3); the facts are
%% those of tab:runs, tab:setup and the tables named, restated in one place.
\paragraph{Coverage notes for the main text.}
Each note below records a run setting that differs from its neighbours in the same sentence of the main text, or a comparison set that is incomplete.
(i) Section~\ref{sec:names_counts}, the count-removal result for GPT-4.1 mini: the names-and-counts ablation ran on its one-call row at temperature $0$ (Table~\ref{tab:cf-names-counts}); its five-draw row of Table~\ref{tab:cf-main} was not rerun under the ablation.
(ii) Section~\ref{sec:names_counts}, the count-removal result for the stronger models: GPT-5.6 Luna at five draws through the raw API and Opus 5 at one draw; GPT-5.6 Sol was not run, dropped by the registration's budget rule (Table~\ref{tab:cf-nc-strong}).
(iii) Section~\ref{sec:lookup}, the summary naming all states: GPT-4.1 mini, Haiku 4.5 and Opus 5 ran it; GPT-5.6 Luna and GPT-5.6 Sol ran the default summary only (Table~\ref{tab:cf-matched}).
(iv) Section~\ref{sec:intent}, the intent crossover: GPT-4.1 mini, Opus 5 and GPT-5.6 Sol ran one draw and Haiku 4.5 and GPT-5.6 Luna five, the body reading their draw-1 column; Sol was added after an interim read of three models' rows (Table~\ref{tab:cf-intent}).
(v) Section~\ref{sec:populations}, the full obligation set: Haiku 4.5, GPT-5.6 Luna and Opus 5 completed it; the GPT-5.6 Sol and Kimi K3 runs stopped at the budget and are not scored as sets (Table~\ref{tab:x4-expansion}).

% R109: two sentences moved verbatim from Section 8 (page-9 gate).
GPT-4.1 mini's blind obligation score is flat on the full population, two values with $604$ of the $661$ questions at one of them, so its indexed-minus-blind difference is not a gain.
Its gain is defined only on the counterfactual set, where it is $+0.22$ and was predicted before the run (Section~\ref{sec:counterfactual}).
It gives six models on the same $84$ questions, the five models with a varying blind obligation score on the full population, and the five capability items registered before the cross-model run.
Across the five models whose blind obligation score carries information, the blind span of $0.3307$ contracts to $0.0389$ when indexed.
The indexed scores sit between $0.8199$ and $0.8588$ across three vendors, so the summary sets the level and the model sets little of it.
The Anthropic pair spans two model generations, so its ordering compares price and capability rather than a within-generation change.
GPT-4.1 mini's obligation row on the full population reads blind $0.4946$ and indexed $0.7339$.
That difference of $+0.2393$ has item-level interval $[+0.1801,+0.2949]$ and $[+0.1552,+0.3237]$ with questions grouped by persona.
The blind score takes two distinct values on the $661$ questions, with or without the rubric's detection step.
The difference is therefore not read as a gain over a blind judgment, and the main text compares the indexed level with the name lookup instead.
On coverage, model scale improves the blind guess, because blind levels include what the question text gives away.
GPT-5.6 Sol's blind FOK reaches $0.8131$ on the $150$-question stratified set and $0.7099$ on the cross-model set.
On the two largest models the coverage gain includes zero (GPT-5.6 Sol $+0.0364$, Kimi K3 $+0.0571$), while the obligation gain excludes zero on both.
GPT-4.1 mini's blind obligation score is constant at $0.0$ on all $84$ items, so its $0.5000$ is an all-tie artifact rather than calibrated uncertainty, and its clustered interval is not reported for the same reason.
Forty-eight distinct values are spanned by GPT-5.6 Luna, which still fails to discriminate.
The GPT-4.1 mini $\Delta$ interval entries carry a dash because those rows are the recorded main-study elicitation, which predates the study's paired-interval machinery, and the persona-clustered interval is shown where defined.

%% R116 (2026-09-19, the author's ruling on the harness rows; reviews/r117/PHASE0_REPORT.md item 6; reviews/NUMBERS_R116.md AUX-R116-HARNESS): the transport disclosure and the row marker.
The GPT-5.6 Luna and GPT-5.6 Sol rows on the cross-model set and on the pooled $66$ structureless questions, marked $^{h}$ here and in Tables~\ref{tab:ladder-predictions}, \ref{tab:inversion} and~\ref{tab:runs}, were elicited in August through the vendor's command-line coding agent (\texttt{codex exec}, reasoning effort low, ephemeral sessions, subscription-billed), which prepends its own system text and tool schemas and passes the frozen system and user texts to the model as one flattened user turn.
No token telemetry survives those runs.
When the full-population runs tried to complete GPT-5.6 Sol through the same agent, two twelve-call probe gates returned no parseable reply, $0$ of $12$ and $0$ of $12$ (the deviation record of those runs), so the agent transport is not one condition with the raw API; the same models' rows on the full populations are raw API calls under the provider default.

The $84$-item cross-model set spans $33$ of the $40$ personas.
Under persona clustering, nine of the ten rows carrying both intervals keep the verdict they had at item level.
The exception is Opus 5 coverage, whose item-level interval excludes zero by $+0.0047$ and whose clustered interval includes it ($[-0.0136,+0.2368]$).
Leave-one-persona-out shows zero sign changes on that row, so the point estimate is stable and the interval is what widens.
Every obligation row survives clustering, including all three models from the other two vendors.

A preregistered expansion re-elicited the blind and indexed conditions on the full FR-Bank populations for every run that completed under the budget stop (Table~\ref{tab:x4-expansion}).
Those populations are obligation $n=661$ with $104$ \pn{} and coverage $n=307$.
The $84$-item cross-model set remains the primary, because its predictions were fixed first.
GPT-4.1 mini's rows are the recorded main-study elicitation, re-derived exactly by the full-population scorer.

\section{Cross-vendor replication record}\label{app:crossvendor}

This appendix carries the five items registered for the three models from vendors other than OpenAI, and one follow-up on the authorship-matched set.
The item-level interval is the registered primary endpoint and the persona-clustered interval is the sensitivity, because that is the convention everywhere in this paper.

\noindent\textbf{Reading the Outcome column.} \textit{held}: every prediction the registration carried was borne out. \textit{failed}: it was not, and the registration's frozen failure text is reported as written. \textit{held / failed}: a registration carrying several predictions, split as the middle column describes. \textit{no prediction}: a method freeze, or a set of branches enumerated in advance with no pass bar, so nothing could hold or fail. \textit{held / undefined}: one prediction held and one named a quantity its population does not define, which the registration had anticipated.\\

On the authorship-matched cell the Haiku 4.5 blind NOK point stays below chance while its interval includes chance, so the matched cell is indeterminate at $n=36$ on whether the inverted prior of XV-1 reflects a property of the model or of question authorship; XV-1's registered verdict is unchanged.
The point falls $0.0073$ below the registered decision boundary of $0.45$, and the values appear in Table~\ref{tab:matched-cell}.

\section{Question-signal audit}\label{app:questionprior}

This appendix records that the question text alone predicts the labels.
That is why no absolute blind level in this paper is a finding, and why the paired gains and the counterfactual set carry the evidence.
Supervised text probes separate \pn{} from non-\pn{} far above chance on every question set, while a zero-shot GPT-4.1 mini reader does not.
The larger $162$-question comparison stays separable (Table~\ref{tab:questionprior}).
The records we deleted were removed by the store's own operation, yet the questions written about them still read differently from the rest.
The structure runs deeper than authorship, because ranking by the recipe used to build each item alone scores $0.9736$ on the cross-model set, above the probe's $0.7972$ (Table~\ref{tab:av-census}).
Six of the eight recipes carry a single class, so rewording cannot make blind levels clean.
Separating construction from authorship needs comparison within a recipe, and only one is large enough ($n=16$).
The same holds for coverage: on hard questions the FOK-positive class coincides with \pn{}, and the coverage-targeted probe scores $0.9662$ pooled, so blind coverage levels carry the same question signal.
Two quantities are free of this signal, because neither depends on the wording of a question.
The paired gains compare identical questions and still measure the summary's added information, and the counterfactual set fixes the question and varies the store (Table~\ref{tab:counterfactual}).
The balanced authorship-matched set ($36$) holds every slot at the same one-third \pn{} rate, and blind NOK is at chance on it for GPT-4.1 mini and Opus 5.

\label{tab:questionprior}

\subsection{Authorship-matched set}\label{app:matched}

The authorship-matched set rewrites every question in one generator voice from each item's entity and slot, so no label is distinguishable by who wrote its question.
It also balances the set so that each slot token carries the same one-third \pn{} rate (Table~\ref{tab:matched-cell}).
Coefficient inspection of the supervised probe first suggested an authorship mismatch, with \pn{} questions bespoke and many negatives reusing generator templates.
The census of Table~\ref{tab:av-census} shows the structure runs deeper than authorship, because six of its eight recipes carry a single class.

This set is not a store counterfactual, and what it holds fixed is stated here because the two designs are easy to confuse.
Within a slot the question is near-identical across labels, differing only in the entity named, and the slot's \pn{} rate is one third by construction.
That is what makes it an authorship and blind-score control.
Each item in a slot belongs to a different persona, so the label co-varies with the entire store rather than with the target's lifecycle state alone.
A counterfactual in the strict sense scores one query against versions of the same store differing only in the target's lifecycle state, which is the design of Appendix~\ref{app:counterfactual}.

Scored post hoc against the PRESENT versions alone, which also hold relevant content, every reader moves by at most $0.03$ from its whole-set value, so the comparison stands where coverage is equal.

This appendix carries the counterfactual set in full.
It gives the whole-set and per-state rows of every reader under the three summaries, the set's earlier thirty-question draw (labelled first form in Table~\ref{tab:counterfactual}), the adverse rows, the coverage rows, the lexical lookup, and the expiry pairs the main text refers to.
The set asks $148$ questions, each of three versions of one store that differ in a single attribute, so $n=444$ with $148$ positives: $88$ supersession, $30$ retraction and $30$ expiry pairs.
The pairs were drawn under predicates fixed before any verdict, and every question was written before any version of the store existed.
A question-only reader scores $0.5000$ on this set as an identity, because the same question is asked of all three versions.
Every gain below is measured against it, with the item-level paired interval first and the interval with questions grouped by persona beside it; where the two disagree the wider one decides.

%% CF-2 P2 HOLDS [MAIN] and P4 (registrations/ADDENDUM_CF2.md section 8): frozen wording ships verbatim.
Under the summary that names only superseded content, Haiku 4.5's judgment on the named state exceeds its judgment on the two counted states by $+0.3237$, interval $[+0.2319,+0.4011]$.
Grouped by persona that interval is $[+0.2612,+0.3851]$.
Opus 5 gives $+0.3295$ with intervals $[+0.2420,+0.4181]$ and $[+0.2556,+0.3875]$, and GPT-4.1 mini's $+0.2948$ (interval $[+0.1875,+0.3905]$) is descriptive.
On this cell the superseded-names lookup reaches $0.7663$ against the smallest reader's $0.7167$ under the superseded-only summary, and the all-states lookup reaches $0.8756$ against Haiku 4.5's $0.8773$ under the all-three-named summary, within tolerance.
Under the summary naming all three states the named-minus-counted difference on Haiku 4.5 falls to $+0.0203$ (interval $[-0.0570,+0.0907]$), so the difference belongs to what the summary names.
The totals-randomized summary permutes the retracted and expired lifecycle totals across the $444$ store versions under a recorded seed, so that no version keeps its own pair and every value keeps its multiplicity; the per-category counts are not touched.
Elsewhere it is byte-identical to the superseded-only summary.
It moves GPT-4.1 mini's whole-set judgment by $+0.0011$, Haiku 4.5's by $+0.0072$ and Opus 5's by $-0.0052$, every interval inside the tolerance band, so no model takes anything from the totals.

\paragraph{Count readers on this set.}
%% R110: the count readers (reviews/NUMBERS_R110.md AUX-R110-A1-CR, -MODELS, -V2R; reviews/v18/tools/a1_count_readers.py; no model call).
Two readers with no generative-model call read the count table alone, so that what a count carries can be measured beside what the models read from it.
%% R118 (ruling 2 of 2026-09-19; reviews/r117/COUNT_READERS_NOTE.md): the two readers read different quantities; the descriptions corrected, the definitions made exact.
The category-aware count reader knows the question's category from the manifest and reads that category's retracted-plus-expired count, a count.
The category-guessing reader assigns the category from the question by the recorded rule of the question-only baseline suite and reads a different quantity at that category, the share of its entries that are superseded, retracted or expired.
A state-aware reader, which knows the state as well as the category, reads the state's own cell.
Under the superseded-only summary the category-aware reader separates the retracted versions at $0.7306$ (item interval $[0.6194,0.8228]$, persona $[0.6900,0.8090]$) and the expired versions at $0.7778$ ($[0.6793,0.8625]$, $[0.7153,0.8680]$), the state-aware reader at $0.8944$ and $0.7778$, and the models on the same items at $0.5283$ to $0.6150$ (Table~\ref{tab:cf-cr}).
The count identifies the version because $26$ of the $30$ retraction pairs and $15$ of the $30$ expiry pairs have a category base count of zero, so the present-negative version's increment reads against zero within its triple.
%% AUX-WP-B-ORDERING (reviews/NUMBERS_WP.md section 8): category-aware reader 0 / 88 / 0 and category-guessing reader 34 / 54 / 0 on the supersession triples.
Within a supersession triple the count table differs only in the superseded column of the target's category, which the category-aware reader does not read, so it ties all $88$ supersession triples; the category-guessing reader's share includes that column, so it orders the $34$ triples whose assigned category is the target's and ties the other $54$.
Read at the manifest category, that share scores $0.8473$ on the whole set ($0.8981$ on supersession, $0.7336$ on retraction, $0.8528$ on expiry): a reader given the question's category, computed after the fact, above GPT-4.1 mini, Haiku 4.5 and Opus 5 on the whole set (Table~\ref{tab:cf-cr}).
A reader of the two lifecycle totals, the lines the totals-randomized summary permutes, reads retraction at $0.6347$ and expiry at $0.6922$ under the superseded-only summary and $0.4044$ and $0.3894$ under the recorded permutation, at chance in expectation over $200$ permutation seeds ($0.4900$ and $0.5065$); the per-category counts are untouched by that permutation, so the category-aware reader is identical under both.

The category-aware reader reads the retracted-plus-expired count at the question's category, the state-aware reader the state's own cell at that category, the totals reader the two lifecycle totals, and the category-guessing reader the share of superseded, retracted and expired entries at the category the question-only suite's rule assigns. The last reader row reads that share at the manifest category: privileged, since the category is given, and added after the fact. The category-aware reader separates the versions on the counted states; every model is below it on both. Descriptive; computed after the fact on the recorded texts and rows.}
\label{tab:cf-cr}
\centering
\tiny
\setlength{\tabcolsep}{2pt}

\paragraph{The moved-count control.}
%% R110: ADDENDUM_V2R_CAT (frozen 257a090; r82_counterfactual/v2rcat/NUMBERS_V2RCAT.md AUX-V2RCAT-LOCK, -REF, -LEAK, -MINI, -H45, -BRANCH, -POSTHOC; D-V2RCAT-1).
%% R114 (2026-09-17): the optional Opus 5 lane (k = 1, Batch; AUX-V2RCAT-OP5, D-V2RCAT-2) added to the prose and tab:v2rcat beside the two registered lanes; the recorded comparator is the k = 5 row.
A second count check starts from the totals-randomized text and, for each of the $60$ counted-state pairs, moves the obligation's own increment in the category row's retracted or expired column from the present-negative version to one non-present-negative sibling under a recorded seed, so that the version carrying the obligation reads the base count and a sibling reads the increment; every other line is unchanged, every name section equals the superseded-only summary's, the renderer lock holds on all $120$ changed texts, and the leakage screen returns $0$ of $30$.
A reader that follows the moved cell falls below chance on that state, the state-aware reader to $0.3028$ on retraction and $0.3611$ on expiry (Table~\ref{tab:v2rcat}).
The registered reading asked whether a model stays within the $0.05$ band of its superseded-only row on both counted states or moves beyond it reliably, and fired as neither for both models, so each row prints as it came out.
Read after the fact against the direction a count reader falls, and labelled so: GPT-4.1 mini moves by $+0.0269$ on retraction and $+0.0611$ on expiry, upward, with both intervals including zero, and Haiku 4.5 by $-0.0072$ and $-0.0453$, so neither registered run established that it followed the count. Opus 5, added as an optional reader, was run once per item ($k=1$, the registered default) against its recorded five-draw superseded-only row. It moves by $-0.1011$ on retraction and $-0.1236$ on expiry, downward, the direction a count reader falls, with each interval excluding zero but reaching the band. The unchanged supersession texts move by $-0.0022$ under the same single draw, which carries draw-count noise against the five-draw row that the two registered runs did not.
%% R129 B1 (2026-09-20): E9 in two sentences (AUX-V2RCAT-A2-P-M2).
GPT-5.6 Luna and GPT-5.6 Sol, run at five draws on the same texts through the raw API against their recorded five-draw rows under the second amendment, fall reliably on expiry, by $-0.1158$ and $-0.1914$, and on retraction by $-0.0664$ and $-0.0322$ with both intervals including zero.
The amendment's one prediction, that each falls reliably on both counted states, failed for both models.

\paragraph{Names against counts on the three stronger models.}
%% R131 (2026-09-20): E2 Amendment 1 (R125 Task 5; AUX-CF2-R125-NC-*).
The same two renderings were run on the three stronger models after the matched metric had been read (Table~\ref{tab:cf-nc-strong}).
Removing every count costs GPT-5.6 Luna $-0.0598$ and Opus 5 $-0.0426$ on the pooled metric, each with both intervals clear of zero, so the prediction that names alone would sit within tolerance of the intact row came out otherwise for Luna and held for Opus 5 at the point.
Counts alone still score reliably below names alone for both, by $-0.0930$ and $-0.1488$, as predicted.
Under the matched metric counts alone read $0.4833$ on retraction for Opus 5 and $0.6667$ on expiry for Luna with the persona interval reaching below $0.50$, so the third prediction came out otherwise for both.
GPT-5.6 Sol was not run: after the sibling-name control's lanes the re-projected cost of its names-and-counts lane would have taken the vendor's total over the cap, and the registration drops the lane in that case.

%% R112 (2026-09-16), post hoc B1 to B4, specified before recomputation (reviews/integration/POSTHOC_SPEC_R112.md, sha256 ae2600fc; reviews/NUMBERS_WP.md sections 8 and 9;
%% reviews/v19/tools/posthoc_b_pnpresent.py, posthoc_b4_intent.py; reviews/v19/results/posthoc_b.json, posthoc_b4.json). No model call.
\paragraph{PN versus PRESENT, and the ordering within triples (post hoc, specified before recomputation).}
The pooled obligation AUROC of Table~\ref{tab:cf-main} scores the \pn{} versions against both other versions, so it mixes two distinctions: \pn{} against \present{}, where both versions hold relevant content, and \pn{} against \absent{}.
A reader that only knows whether the store covers the question scores $1$ on \pn{} and \present{} and $0$ on \absent{}, which gives $0.75$ pooled on this set and $0.5000$ on the \pn{}-versus-\present{} comparison, because it ties every triple.
Table~\ref{tab:pnpresent} separates the two for every reader, summary and state, with the pooled value equal to the mean of the two (asserted to four decimals, the negative groups being equal in size), and counts, per triple, whether the reader scores the \pn{} version above, equal to, or below the \present{} version.
The largest move from the pooled value on the whole set, any reader, is $0.0230$, so the model-versus-lookup comparison of Section~\ref{sec:lookup} is the same on the harder comparison: the name lookup reads $0.7663$, GPT-4.1 mini $0.7056$, Haiku 4.5 $0.7493$ and Opus 5 $0.8370$.
The strict-win fraction counts ties as failures and is not a tie-adjusted ranking accuracy; GPT-4.1 mini's single call returns coarse probabilities, so its tie count is large ($57$ of $148$ triples on the whole set), and the interval to read is the persona-grouped one, since triples share personas.
Where the summary only counts a state, the two lookups tie every retraction and expiry triple under the superseded-only summary (their lists do not differ), the count readers win most of them, and the models split between ties and small numbers of wins and losses; naming the states turns most of those ties into wins for every reader.
The expected outcomes written before the run held for the name lookup and GPT-4.1 mini (supersession above $0.85$ and $0.70$; retraction and expiry within tolerance of $0.50$ under the superseded-only summary) and for the all-states lookup (above $0.70$ on both counted states); they did not hold for Opus 5, which reads $0.5689$ on retraction and $0.6128$ on expiry against the \present{} versions, nor for Haiku 4.5 on expiry ($0.5544$), nor for the count readers, which the pooled result had already shown to separate the counted states.

The same store judged under two intents cannot be read from the recorded rows: each question carries one constructed intent and the \textsc{change} intent is never \pn{}, so any comparison across intents would vary intent with the label; the crossover below builds that test with new questions.
Two things can be read instead (post hoc, specified before recomputation).
An intent-aware name lookup, whose score is set to zero on the $105$ \textsc{change}-intent questions of the full obligation set (superseded content is licensed under that intent), reads $0.7957$ against the plain lookup's $0.7484$, $+0.0473$ with intervals $[+0.0331,+0.0654]$ and $[+0.0284,+0.0689]$; the all-states lookup moves from $0.7806$ to $0.8255$ under the same rule plus a zero on the $9$ past-intent questions whose best match is an expired name.
The items on which intent alone decides the label, \textsc{change}-intent licensed questions whose own slot holds a superseded record, number $4$ of the $45$ on the full obligation set, below the floor of five the specification set for a reading, so no monitor comparison is printed on them.

\paragraph{The matched metric.}
%% R131 (2026-09-20): R125 Task 1 (AUX-CF2-R125-*) and Task 2 (AUX-CF2-R125-T2-*); the pooled Figure 3 moves here.
The counterfactual set fixes the question and varies the store, so the comparison the design supports is within a triple; the pooled AUROC of Table~\ref{tab:cf-main} also orders each positive against the negatives of every other question.
The matched AUROC of Section~\ref{sec:setup} is $(W+T/2)/n$ over the triples, the \pn{} version against the \present{} version, with a tie counting half; it was defined after every pooled row was known, and every value here is post hoc.
Table~\ref{tab:cf-matched} gives it for every reader of Table~\ref{tab:cf-main} with the triple-level and the persona-grouped interval, whole set and per state; Table~\ref{tab:cf-classes} decomposes the pooled AUROC by pair class; Table~\ref{tab:cf-matched-vs} gives the paired differences against the lookups and the count readers; Table~\ref{tab:cf-matched-ablations} the ablations; Figure~\ref{fig:perstate-pooled} is the pooled version of Figure~\ref{fig:perstate}.
Under this metric a tie is half a win, so the readers that never lose a triple sit on their tie counts: the name lookup ties $66$ of $148$ triples and reads $0.7770$, and the guessing count reader ties $83$ and reads $0.7196$, while a model with fine-grained scores rarely ties and pays for every loss (GPT-4.1 mini's five-draw row loses $33$ triples against its one-draw row's $10$, so it reads $0.7264$ against $0.7399$).
The three claims of Section~\ref{sec:counterfactual} whose reading changes under it are the count readers on the counted states, where GPT-5.6 Luna, GPT-5.6 Sol and Opus 5 are no longer reliably below the guessing reader (Table~\ref{tab:cf-matched-vs}); the moved count, which Luna and Sol follow reliably as well as Opus 5 (Table~\ref{tab:cf-matched-ablations}); and the hybrid reader (Table~\ref{tab:cf-hybrid}).
One reliability changes: Opus 5 under the all-states summary exceeds the all-states lookup by $+0.0304$ with intervals $[-0.0068,+0.0643]$ and $[-0.0038,+0.0648]$, not reliable, where the pooled difference of Table~\ref{tab:cf-main} is; its first draw alone reads $+0.0372$ with both intervals clear of zero.
What the matched comparison does not remove is list length: the random-token lookup reads $0.6453$ on it ($0.7443$ on supersession) because the \pn{} version's list is one name longer than the \present{} version's, and Table~\ref{tab:cf-emptiness} measures that component; the sibling-name control below is built to remove it.

Middle panel: the random-token control (every name replaced by a random token, the list's length and emptiness kept) on the counterfactual set under both summaries, pooled and matched, and on the full obligation set (no triples, so no matched row; each state's positives against all $557$ negatives). Lower panel: the name lookup minus the random-token control, pooled with the item-level and persona-grouped intervals and matched with the triple-level and persona-grouped intervals ($^{*}$ both exclude zero). Under the default summary $0.60$ of the lookup's $0.77$ pooled and $0.65$ of its $0.78$ matched is reachable with no name meaning anything; the sibling-name control (Table~\ref{tab:cf-sibling}) removes that component.}

\paragraph{The intent crossover.}
%% R123 (2026-09-19): the crossover of reviews/r121 (registrations/ADDENDUM_CF2_INTENT_CROSSOVER.md, 648d671; Amendment 1, 2c441a7; NUMBERS_CF2.md section 14d) and R122's T4
%% (reviews/r122/registrations/ADDENDUM_R122_CF2_P1.md, 77bc0d9; reviews/r122/NUMBERS_R122.md AUX-R122-P1-*). Every number is the tables' (ledger_r123_tables.py).
The crossover asks each in-scope supersession pair of the set its recorded present-tense question and a second question about how the attribute has changed, and each expiry pair a question about the past event, of the same two store versions, so that the same stale record is forbidden under the original question and licensed under the crossed one (Table~\ref{tab:cf-intent}).
The crossed questions were drafted by GPT-4.1 mini from the attribute path and the time sense alone, never from any stored value, screened for value leakage, and read by us before any monitor call; five pairs were dropped and two took a second drafting seed.
Two rubrics were used: the main-study rubric, and one that states that the obligation depends on the question's intent.
The name lookup, a keyword-intent lookup over a fixed cue list, and an oracle-intent lookup that reads the constructed intent are the readers with no generative-model call.
Three predictions were written before any call.
The first, that every model would sit within tolerance of $0.50$ on the same-store contrast under the main-study rubric, came out otherwise: four models sit below it and Opus 5 above it.
The second, that Opus 5 and Haiku 4.5 would separate the intents under the intent-explicit rubric, came out otherwise for Haiku 4.5.
The third, that the name lookup would sit within tolerance of $0.50$, came out as written.
GPT-5.6 Sol's prediction, made after an interim read of three models' rows, that it separates the intents under the intent-explicit rubric, came out as written.
Under the intent-explicit rubric the counterfactual set itself was rerun for GPT-5.6 Luna at five draws and GPT-4.1 mini at one (Table~\ref{tab:cf-p1}).
Each reads within $0.02$ of its main-rubric row ($0.8531$ against $0.8417$; $0.7253$ against $0.7167$).
Luna keeps its per-state order and a reliable margin over the name lookup ($+0.0867$, intervals $[+0.0447,+0.1302]$ and $[+0.0562,+0.1219]$); GPT-4.1 mini's two counted states swap order (expiry $0.5489$ to $0.4758$, retraction $0.5283$ to $0.5456$), neither move reliable, which is a failed prediction.

\paragraph{Score resolution.}
%% R110: AUX-R110-A3 (reviews/v18/tools/a3_single_draw.py; no model call).
The models scored from five calls carry finer score resolution than GPT-4.1 mini's single call, and the margin over the lookup does not depend on it.
Opus 5's first draw alone reads $0.8400$ on this set ($53$ distinct values against $264$ after averaging), $+0.0736$ above the name lookup with intervals $[+0.0298,+0.1223]$ and $[+0.0388,+0.1129]$, and its five draws span $0.0059$; Haiku 4.5's first draw reads $0.7555$ ($17$ values), $-0.0108$ against the lookup with both intervals including zero; GPT-4.1 mini's single draw takes $12$ values.
%% R123: R122 T3 (reviews/r122/registrations/ADDENDUM_R122_MINI_K5.md, 77bc0d9; reviews/r122/NUMBERS_R122.md AUX-R122-K5-*; the supersession margin from the same rows, tab:sup-margins).
At five draws with the provider's default temperature GPT-4.1 mini reads $0.76$ under the superseded-only summary and $0.83$ under the all-states summary, within $0.05$ of its one-draw rows, with the tied triples falling from $51$ to $6$ (Table~\ref{tab:mini-k5}).
Table~\ref{tab:cf-main} prints those rows.
At five draws the model sits within tolerance of the name lookup on the whole set ($-0.0016$, both intervals including zero) and on supersession ($-0.0429$, intervals $[-0.0898,+0.0006]$ and $[-0.1001,+0.0035]$), where its one-draw row is reliably below (Table~\ref{tab:sup-margins}).
On the full obligation set the first draws read $0.8156$ (Haiku 4.5), $0.8335$ (Opus 5) and $0.8141$ (GPT-5.6 Luna) against averaged rows of $0.8210$, $0.8327$ and $0.8182$.
GPT-4.1 mini's repeated calls return identical scores on $46$ of $50$ questions.

The registered texts for this set (Appendix~\ref{app:registration}) read as follows.
%% CF-2 P1 HOLDS [MAIN] and P3 HOLDS [MAIN] (registrations/ADDENDUM_CF2.md section 8; AUX-CF2-MINI-V2, AUX-CF2-COUNTED): frozen wording ships
%% verbatim; moved here from Section 5 under registrations/AMENDMENT_MAIN_PLACEMENT.md (R105, 2026-09-14; pending the author's ratification).
On $148$ questions written before any version of the store existed, and asked of three versions of the same store that differ in one attribute each, the smallest model's obligation judgment reaches $0.7167$ against the $0.5$ a question-only reader attains here, with an interval of $[+0.1494,+0.2846]$. The gain survives where the question cannot carry the label.
Naming retracted and expired content lifts Haiku 4.5's judgment on those states from $0.5638$ to $0.8649$ on the store-counterfactual set ($60$ questions), a paired gain of $+0.3010$ with interval $[+0.2268,+0.3721]$.

The adverse rows are reported in full, because a row that runs against the paper's reading is evidence too.
The all-states lookup exceeds GPT-4.1 mini on the whole set under every summary ($+0.1589$, $+0.0789$, $+0.1577$), each beyond the $\pm0.05$ tolerance.
It exceeds Haiku 4.5 under the two summaries that do not name the counted states ($+0.1190$, $+0.1118$), also beyond tolerance, and Opus 5 under those two summaries within it ($+0.0351$, $+0.0403$).
The name lookup exceeds GPT-4.1 mini and Haiku 4.5 under the superseded-only summary within tolerance ($+0.0496$, $+0.0097$).
By state, the name lookup ($0.9223$ on supersession) exceeds GPT-4.1 mini and Haiku 4.5 under every summary and trails Opus 5.
The all-states lookup ($0.7975$ on retraction, $0.8711$ on expiry) exceeds every reader on the two counted states under the summaries that only count them.
The whole name-map lookup ($0.5758$ on retraction, $0.5919$ on expiry) exceeds GPT-4.1 mini on both counted states under every summary except retraction under the all-three summary.
That is what a reader of the whole map should do when the counted names sit in that map.
Table~\ref{tab:sup-margins} gives each model's supersession-only margin over the name lookup with both intervals.
%% R124 B2 (2026-09-19): reviews/NUMBERS_R124.md AUX-R124-OP5-V3-MARGINS (reviews/v21/tools/margins_r124.py, the recorded rows, no model call).
Under the all-states summary Opus 5's margin over the all-states lookup is reliable on the whole set ($+0.0323$) but on no single state: supersession $+0.0324$, retraction $+0.0125$ and expiry $+0.0556$, each with an interval that includes zero.
